The August Risk Report wrote that "our internal AI R&D efforts are significantly faster than they would be without AI assistance, but not yet by a factor of 2," put the onset of "meaningful acceleration" in early-to-mid 2025, attributed that onset to causes other than AI, and added that AI models "have been a key factor in the faster trends continuing" [Anthropic, 2026b]. The Opus 5.5 system card of September 22 reported no "sustained AI-attributable 2× acceleration," said "some of these measures have moved," and said the model "provides meaningful acceleration to AI R&D efforts in many circumstances" [Anthropic, 2026c]. METR's embedded team, the only outside party with a figure, estimated "~1.5X overall acceleration in capabilities due to AI (i.e. 1.5 years in 1 year), with perhaps 30% chance of 2X acceleration," over a period it did not state [METR, 2026].

Google DeepMind's Gemini 4 Argon report fitted its capability index and found 0.091 points a month before April 1, 2026 and 0.118 after, not a significant difference, and "no significant end-to-end acceleration of AI R&D, AI-driven or otherwise" [Google DeepMind, 2026]; Google's chief executive said the same week that "you have RSI loops along with AI researchers in various aspects of model development" and, as coverage reports a second interview, "I would expect the next 12 months to see continued acceleration because of RSI" [Kantrowitz, 2026a; Rijo, 2026]. [Updated, version 1.28: the sentence is verbatim in Alphabet's Q3 earnings-call transcript of October 28, in prepared remarks, as "the next twelve months"; it is primary there; whether he also said it on the Sources podcast remains as coverage; see 8.43.] OpenAI has published inputs and no multiplier: 3.1 agent-workdays per human workday, more than $600 a day of inference for the median researcher, more than half of successful four-to-eight-hour tasks needing intervention, and the sentence "the overall pace of progress likely won't keep pace with these specific metrics" [OpenAI, 2026]. Those are the measurements. Around them this week sat four accounts and one claim: Kelsey Piper's report from a briefing at Anthropic, Jack Clark's lecture texts in Tokyo and Bangkok, Sam Altman's July sentence that "we are now, like, in the singularity" with its academic rebuttal, Recursive's release of a model it says its research system helped build, and Goodfire's essay that neural networks are "already clean."

**How a 4× per person becomes below 2× for the lab.** The card states the mechanism, and it is the same one Section 5.2 takes from Aghion, Jones and Jones: when the automated part of a process speeds up, the whole process is paced by the parts that do not. Anthropic writes that "productivity uplift on individual tasks does not translate one-for-one into acceleration of research progress. Compute is also a key ingredient, as promising ideas need to be de-risked at scale. Our best estimates of the elasticity of progress to researcher output, combined with the observed uplift, yield an overall progress multiplier below 2×" [Anthropic, 2026a, Section 2.3.6]. The same card records what happens to the loudest positive claims when they are checked: "In the initial weeks of internal use, several specific claims were made that Claude Mythos Preview had independently delivered a major research contribution. When we followed up on each claim, it appeared that the contribution was real, but smaller or differently shaped than initially understood … In some cases what looked like autonomous discovery was, on inspection, reliable execution of a human-specified approach." The card says it reports this "not to diminish the model, but because it is the concrete form that the gap between productivity uplift and measurable progress acceleration takes in practice." That sentence is the whole of this subsection's argument, written by the lab in April. The per-person half and the lab-level half are both true, and the labs' written bar applies to the second.

Placement. The 4× is rung 1 and rung 2 output per person, self-reported; the "below 2×" and METR's 1.5× are the first measurements of the rung-4 quantity, whether AI-produced work makes the next model arrive sooner, and both read under the bar. It is accurate to say rung 4 is not demonstrated at the labs' threshold. It is not accurate to say nothing is measured there: the one outside estimate is 1.5 years of progress in a year, with a 30% chance that the bar is already met, and the lab's own estimate is a multiplier above one and below two. The report's ladder row for rung 4 reads "Not demonstrated, not claimed" (7.1); the proposed note is in the summary part below. No date.

**Anthropic's August Risk Report, read for the half the report dropped.** 9.2 summarized the Risk Report as putting measured acceleration at "less than a factor of 2," mostly from causes other than AI. The document's sentence is longer. "Our leading indicators point to a picture of meaningful acceleration starting in early-to-mid 2025, though by less than a factor of 2. We are fairly confident in attributing the acceleration in 2025 to factors other than our use of AI models, though we also believe that our AI models have been a key factor in the faster trends continuing through the coverage date. We conclude that the overall pace of progress remains below the RSP threshold of a doubling over pre-AI-assisted rates of progress attributable primarily to AI R&D automation, but even with our additional sources of data this conclusion is uncertain and subject to some lag" (the sentence continues: so that very recent acceleration would be hard to measure) [Anthropic, 2026b, Section 3.5.2]. The threat-model table says the same in one line: "We believe our internal AI R&D efforts are significantly faster than they would be without AI assistance, but not yet by a factor of 2 (though we are uncertain and measurement is difficult)." And in its forward look: "we may cross this threshold in the coming year" [Anthropic, 2026b, Sections 3.3, 3.9]. The Opus 5.5 card's conclusion adds a sentence 8.25 did not quote: "We think it provides meaningful acceleration to AI R&D efforts in many circumstances, and is somewhat more capable in this domain than the models described in our previous system cards" [Anthropic, 2026c, Section 2.3.7].

Placement. These are the lab's own sentences carrying both halves, and the report's compression kept the negative clause of each. The correction is to the report's reading, not to its verdict: below 2×, with AI "a key factor" in the acceleration continuing, uncertain, lagging, and possibly crossed within a year, is what Anthropic wrote. T1 is not triggered; the sentence that would trigger it is a declaration, and Anthropic has made none. H4, that labs know more than they publish, gets the qualifier the card gives it: the "most direct piece of evidence" for the negative reading is interviews whose details are "research-sensitive," shown to external reviewers and not to the public. The incentive runs both ways in one document: a lab preparing a listing has a reason to say its models accelerate its work and a reason, under its own policy, to say the threshold is not crossed. The card does both.

**Google against Google.** The Argon report's first finding, "no significant end-to-end acceleration of AI R&D, AI-driven or otherwise," has its numbers above. Two interviews with Sundar Pichai in the same week read the same facts the other way up. On the Big Technology Podcast, in a transcript Alex Kantrowitz posted on October 20 behind a paywall and summarized without one on October 23, Pichai said: "We are pretty far from the end state of RSI." And: "The right way to describe it is, you have RSI loops along with AI researchers in various aspects of model development." And: "You almost have agents helping AI researchers in some automated way work together to build these models." In the free part of the transcript: "The frontier models will be better at developing the next set of models in an era of recursive self-improvement" [Kantrowitz, 2026a; 2026b]. On Alex Heath's Sources podcast, posted to YouTube on October 21 and not watched here, Pichai said, as PPC Land reports, "I would expect the next 12 months to see continued acceleration because of RSI," described researchers "co-working with the models in a deep way," said compute allocation is "being made by uh humans in the loop including me," and, in paraphrase, that models propose training recipes and assess checkpoints, with "a lot of humans in the loop" kept for safety [Rijo, 2026, as coverage]. The watcher's digest of October 23 attributed all of this to one Benzinga article; the Benzinga page returned 403 to every fetch, and the remarks come from two interviews with two hosts.

Placement. Pichai's "RSI loops" is the loose usage Brin used a week earlier (8.36) and Amodei in September (8.12): AI helping to build the next model, which is rung 1 to 2 as described, with "end state" reserved for the strict definition and placed "pretty far" off. His expectation of "continued acceleration because of RSI" over twelve months is a forecast of the rung-4 quantity by the chief executive of the company whose safety report measured it as flat. [Updated, version 1.28: restated to investors on October 28, preceded by "AI is helping us increase our training and launch velocity"; see 8.43.] Both can be true: the report's index is a lagging measure of released frontier models with a wide interval after April, and the chief executive is describing what he sees inside. The disagreement is the one this subsection is about, inside one company, in one week. C4 gains "RSI loops" as a phrase. For H5, a chief executive in a launch month describing acceleration while his safety organization publishes "no significant" acceleration is the pattern 9.1 names, with the institution's number lower than the principal's sentence. No date; "the next 12 months" is a direction, not a milestone. No Japanese coverage of either interview was found.

**OpenAI's ledger against OpenAI's test.** The September 6 post gives inputs and declines to give a multiplier: "AI research is a complex process with many potential bottlenecks, so the overall pace of progress likely won't keep pace with these specific metrics. But on the whole, these findings are consistent with the broader impression many of us have internally that agentic tools are meaningfully accelerating research progress. People still set our research priorities, judge which ideas and results to pursue, and decide whether to scale, pause, or deploy systems" [OpenAI, 2026]. The figures are the ones 8.10 records: the median researcher "using more than $600 per day of inference at API prices" by mid-August and the 90th percentile "more than $7,000 of tokens per day"; "3.1 agent-workdays of effort for every workday of human labor"; "High-level planning still remains a minimal fraction of agent output tokens"; "over half of successful 4-8 hour tasks involved 1 or more interventions"; and the milestone, "we have now reached the goal … of having an automated research intern," defined at declaration. The bar OpenAI has written for itself is in its Preparedness Framework: High is reached when the model's impact equals giving every researcher "a highly performant mid-career research engineer assistant, relative to those researchers' 2024 baseline," and Critical when the model is "capable of recursively self improving (i.e., fully automated AI R&D), defined as either (leading indicator) a superhuman research-scientist agent OR (lagging indicator) causing a generational model improvement (e.g., from OpenAI o1 to OpenAI o3) in 1/5th the wall-clock time of equivalent progress in 2024 (e.g., sped up to just 4 weeks) sustainably for several months" [OpenAI, 2025, Table 1]. No OpenAI model is rated High (8.1, 8.28, 8.35), and no OpenAI document states a measured multiplier or a generation time.

Placement. OpenAI is the lab with the most input data and the least output measurement. Its own sentence says the pace of progress is below the pace of its metrics and above zero; the framework's lagging indicator is a wall-clock test that the company has the data to run and has not published. T5 moves in 8.38: the pause that began on September 20 ended on October 1, announced on October 23. For T4, the ledger is a published series of inputs, not of gains. Rung 2 under direction, by OpenAI's own classifier. No date beyond March 2028.

**Piper inside Anthropic.** On October 21 Kelsey Piper published "What I saw inside Anthropic," subtitled "The leading AI lab has hopes, not plans," in The Argument [Piper, 2026a, https://www.theargumentmag.com/p/what-i-saw-inside-anthropic]. She and Jerusalem Demsas attended a briefing at Anthropic's San Francisco headquarters in early October on AI-enabled biological weapons risk; Demsas declined the non-disclosure agreement and the host "open[ed] the proceedings by saying that anyone who wanted could get the NDA voided." On the charts: "Some of the data on the charts that Anthropic showed us was confidential internal data, but it was all in support of a thesis that is not secret — and which, in fact, I had thought 'everybody' already knew, though from the nervous questions of my fellow travelers, I learned that some of the intricacies still came as a surprise." Anthropic classifies its internal work by asking whether a human did it unassisted, whether "a human give AI instructions that the AI followed in order to do it," or whether "a human give AI extremely minimal guidance that the AI then turned into a detailed project spec and completed," and "The share of the latter categories is marching upwards." Then: "The labs are making extensive use of each generation of AI to train its successor, and they intend for AIs to do nearly all of this work eventually. This allows them to expand their effective 'workforce' from a few thousand human employees to tens, even hundreds of thousands of AI employees. It generates far too much data for any human to interpret, leaving us reliant on the AIs for analysis. 'Recursive self-improvement' is what the labs call the point at which Claude can independently build the next, more powerful Claude. Anthropic co-founder Jack Clark has said we will likely achieve it next year." The piece mentions Anthropic's "trillion-dollar valuation" once and the IPO not at all [Piper, 2026a].

The companion piece the watcher paired with it is older: "Losing control of AI is actually the plan," September 8, which cites a talk by co-founder Tom Brown saying "the vast majority of the work Anthropic does is internal software engineering," quotes Anthropic's own post, "At Anthropic, we are delegating a growing share of AI development to AI systems themselves," reports OpenAI's research compute for internal coding inference up "100-fold" over six months, and writes that "Research teams of hundreds of thousands of AI agents will be doing all the work to improve the next generation" [Piper, 2026b, https://www.theargumentmag.com/p/losing-control-of-ai-is-actually].

Placement. This is a journalist's account of charts she was shown and may not reproduce, and the charts are the three-category classification 8.18 documents from Anthropic's own index: the "latter categories" are AL3 and AL4, and "marching upwards" is the 1% to 26% series. It is independent confirmation that the index Anthropic published is the one it shows visitors, and it adds no figure. "Tens, even hundreds of thousands of AI employees" matches the 30,000 concurrent agents of 8.18 in order of magnitude and is a plan, not a count. "Jack Clark has said we will likely achieve it next year" is Piper's paraphrase of a 2027 date, consistent with Import AI 476 and with his Tokyo lecture below; Clark's own texts say "more likely" and "above 50%," not "likely achieve." Rung 1 to 2 as reported, with the strict rung-4 definition correctly stated as the labs' and not claimed. The incentive is stated by Piper herself: a lab that has briefed outsiders in order to be written about, at a trillion-dollar valuation, and that she thinks "would reject the claim that it fears losing its trillion-dollar valuation." For H3, a company organizing its own disclosure; for H1, a company disclosing that the loop is its plan. No date beyond "next year." No Japanese coverage found.

**Clark in Tokyo and Bangkok.** Two lecture texts. The Bangkok one, Import AI 477 of October 19, is the prepared text of his IMF lecture of October 17 and the verified scan's reading of it holds: it says "I think it's evident we're in the early stages of the singularity," "The singularity is here," and that AI "is contributing to its own development," that "the whole pace of progress of not just AI, but science as a whole, will speed up," and that this includes "certainly AI research itself, which could have an explosive effect on innovation"; it does not use "recursive" and gives no 2027 and no probability [Clark, 2026a, https://jack-clark.net/2026/10/19/import-ai-477-in-which-i-tell-the-imf-the-singularity-has-begun/].

The Tokyo one is new to this report and corrects 8.36, which recorded that Import AI 476 gave no probability and that no Japanese coverage was found. ITmedia AI+ reported on October 14 that on October 13, at a lecture hosted by the University of Tokyo's Matsuo-Iwasawa laboratory, Clark said: 自らの後継システムを訓練できるほど強力なAIが2027年に登場する確率は50％以上, up from 年初に公表した「2027年に30％、2028年末までに60％」という予想. He separated capability from use: AIがAI自身を改善する「再帰的自己改善」（RSI）は一度始めれば戻れない“片道切符”（本人の表現では「one-way door」）であり、来年それを実行する可能性は「ゼロに近い」, and whether to run it is 「Anthropicのような企業だけでなく、政府や他の人々が決める選択だ」. He cited the index: 26年に入って、社内コードベースの作業の26％がAI主導になった, called it 「RSIの外側のループ」, said 「27年にRSIを実現できるようになる確率が上がったことで、AIにブレーキが必要だという確信が強まった」, and that 「この夏の教訓は、自動化システムを観測する仕組みも、止める仕組みも、まだ作れていないということだ」 [村田, 2026, https://www.itmedia.co.jp/aiplus/article/2610/14/2000002262/]. No recording or text of the Tokyo lecture was found on the laboratory's or Clark's pages. NHK ran an item on October 16 at 21:12 headlined 米アンソロピック共同創業者を直撃「AIがAIを開発する」とは？; its body could not be read and it is listed as not read [NHK, 2026].

Placement. The Tokyo figure is the first probability Clark has given since May, and it is above one half for the capability in 2027, with the use of it put near zero for 2027 by the same speaker. That is the sharpest statement of the two halves by any lab principal in this record: the loop could close next year, and his company puts the chance of running it next year near zero. It is a forecast and not a measurement; it is rung 4 as capability expected, with the "outer loop" he describes being the AL4 share of 8.18, rung 2. Q73 asked whether Clark restated the date or a probability after Import AI 476; he did, in Tokyo, four days before Bangkok, where he gave neither. For H1 the "one-way door" and "brakes" are the pacing position restated to students; for H5 a co-founder raising his odds in a lecture series while his company prepares a listing is what the hypothesis predicts. ITmedia's 社内コードベースの作業 renders what the index measures as model R&D work (8.18); the reporter's phrasing or the speaker's is not known. No date inside December–March.

**Altman's July sentence, and the rebuttal.** The brief asked whether Section 8 carries Sam Altman's line from July; it does not, and it is recorded here because the "singularity" sentences of Clark and Altman are now read together in coverage. On the Relentless podcast episode "Sam Altman - How to Start a Startup," published on YouTube on July 25, 2026 by the channel of Ti Morse (484,179 views at read), Altman said, as Fortune reports, "We are now, like, in the singularity," and "This is the moment" [Relentless, 2026; Quiroz-Gutierrez, 2026, https://fortune.com/2026/07/27/sam-altman-ai-singularity-elon-musk-openai-hugging-face-breach/]. The episode was not listened to for this revision; the quotation is as Fortune and The Conversation give it, and the two agree. Elon Musk replied on X the same weekend, "We are in the Singularity." Kai Riemer and Sandra Peter of the University of Sydney answered in The Conversation on July 31: Vernor Vinge "defined it in 1993" as "a point at which machine intelligence exceeds human intelligence and begins improving itself," "It is recursive: the system improves itself over and over again," and today's models meet neither condition, because after pre-training "the network itself is frozen in time," every weight "is fixed," and "The model that broke into Hugging Face was identical afterwards to what it had been before." They concede the half this subsection is about: "AI models take part in improving some of their system's components" [Riemer and Peter, 2026, https://theconversation.com/sam-altman-says-were-in-the-singularity-with-ai-heres-why-hes-wrong-288514].

Placement. Altman's sentence is the loose definition with no figure, two days after his company disclosed the Hugging Face incident (8.9); the rebuttal is the strict definition with the mechanism named, weights frozen after training, and its concession is the measured record above. Neither gives a date. C4: "singularity" now joins "RSI" as a word used by two chief executives and a co-founder for a condition their own instruments rate below their own bars. The Information's report of October 19 that Altman described three "endgames" to staff, one of them a single firm reaching RSI first, remains paraphrase with no transcript and no OpenAI comment, as the verified scan of October 21 recorded; it is not quoted here.

**Recursive's Flint.** Recursive, the company Richard Socher and Tim Rocktäschel founded, released its first model on October 21: "Introducing Flint," listed on its updates page under that date, the page itself stamped October 23 [Recursive, 2026a, https://recursive.com/updates/introducing-recursive-flint]. The company's own words: "Building Flint was a practical test of Recursive's Eureka Machine"; "Recursive's Eureka Machine automates the full research loop"; "Our Eureka Machine helped develop nearly every part of Flint's model-building stack"; "Our Eureka Machine contributed throughout Flint's development, from data, optimizers, and training recipes" onward; "Our small team of people and agents built it from scratch in under four months." The named contributions are an architecture search "for an architecture that would improve throughput on local hardware," "Starting from a baseline with 35 billion total parameters and 3 billion active parameters"; a search for "quantization recipes" that "quantized the output layer (lm_head) to 4 bits"; "an offline distillation stage in which the quantized model trains on its own pre-sampled responses," which "nearly halved the number of training steps compared with our quantization-aware training baseline"; and "specialized kernels for low-bit projections, MoE operations, attention, and prompt processing," including "a Metal megakernel that fuses the entire decode forward pass" on Apple chips. Flint "has 40 billion total parameters and activates just 2.5 billion per token," is on Hugging Face "under the Apache 2.0 license," and "leads MoE models with fewer than 50 billion total parameters on agentic coding benchmarks" (SWE-Bench Pro v2, Terminal-Bench 2.1, SWE-Bench Verified), on "our benchmarks," run by the company. The post's last line of purpose: "Our goal at Recursive is to build recursively self-improving superintelligence to automate knowledge discovery" [Recursive, 2026a].

The company's June 11 post reports the system's earlier results on three public research benchmarks: NanoChat Autoresearch validation bits per byte 0.9372 to 0.9109, NanoGPT Speedrun 79.7 to 77.5 seconds, SOL-ExecBench 0.699 to 0.754 [Recursive, 2026b, https://recursive.com/updates/first-steps-toward-automated-ai-research]. The money is from May: "$650 million at a $4.65 billion valuation," led by GV and Greycroft with AMD Ventures and Nvidia, when the company "hasn't published any concrete technical results" [Schreiner, 2026, https://the-decoder.com/ai-startup-recursive-emerges-from-stealth-with-650-million-to-build-self-improving-ai/]. Rocktäschel's reported claim that the company can build recursive self-improving AI "within roughly two years" appears in a Crypto Briefing item of June 11 that cites no interview, outlet or event; no primary was found and it is [UNVERIFIED] [Crypto Briefing, 2026]. WIRED's review of October 21 exists on the company's page as a link and could not be opened here.

Placement. This is rung 3 as a company's claim: an automated research system proposing and testing architecture, quantization, distillation and kernel changes, with a verifier (benchmark score, throughput) at each step, which is the regime Section 3.4 says the method works in. The watcher wrote that the system "automated most of the model's development, including training method, architecture, chip code"; the primary says "nearly every part," names the parts, and says people and agents built it together, without saying how the work divided or how much time the system saved. It is not a frontier lab, the model is a 40-billion-parameter local coding model, the benchmarks are the company's, and the company sells the thesis it is testing. T3 and T4 are untouched: no venue, no series, one halving of training steps on one quantization stage. C4 gains the title of its mission and, in coverage, "AI-built." No date; the two-year claim has no source. No Japanese press coverage was found; a May explainer on a Japanese company blog covers the funding [AI Revolution, 2026].

**The outside measurements of research capability, and a working scientist.** The report already holds the outside record and it is restated here so it sits beside the lab numbers. Kirgis et al. (3.3): frontier agents given six days and about $3,000 per paper "completed all of the engineering without human help, yet could not make substantial progress towards answering the research questions." Epoch's InnovationEval (8.35): about 15% of a human method's gain. TasteVal (8.35): 2.30× an expert baseline on eight easy-to-verify experiment-design tasks, doubling every three months. The pattern is the verification hierarchy of 3.4: strongest where the check is exact and cheap, weakest where the question is open.

One account from outside AI research belongs here because the commissioner used it in the talk and the report already carries it. The anonymized memo "Mathematics, papers and archives after AI" records a conversation of late September with a mathematician who "is producing about one 80-page paper a week," for whom "What limits him now is where he can publish," whose agents produce "a 12-page referee report with 30 suggested improvements" in about five minutes and rebuild a paper against a review in a day with about 100 agents, and who spends "roughly 90%" of his system's tokens on adversarial review; "The mathematician is not named, and details that would identify him are left out or generalized" [Ito, 2026, https://joi.ito.com/memos/ai-math-archive/]. Separately, the commissioner reports from his own correspondence of September 29 that the same mathematician produced a 20-page referee report for a journal in about an hour where the task had taken three weeks, and that the journal's footnote asks referees not to use AI. That is the commissioner's account, not checked by this revision, and it carries no name.

Placement. These are rung 3 in a field with an exact verifier, mathematics, and the bottleneck the memo names is the same one Section 5 names for AI research: not ideas, but the parts of the process that do not speed up, here review and publication. It is the positive half of the record at the level of one scientist's output, and it says nothing about the labs' development cycle. No tracker item; no date.

**Goodfire: "Neural Networks Are Already Clean."** On October 15 Nick Cammarata of Goodfire, with Curt Tigges and Tarun Gogineni, published an essay at already-clean.goodfire.com, labelled "A working hypothesis" and "an informal research essay." Its claim: "The cause of polysemanticity in early model organisms wasn't superposition, but several ordinary, mostly fixable issues. We can now understand most neurons in models up to trillions of parameters." On Qwen3-32B, in "preliminary audits," "roughly 90% of neuron poles have one main job," "another ~7% have two related jobs," and "only ~2-4% are not yet understood," with the caveat "these are relatively small samples"; "Highly speculative: this might be most of what we need"; the language-model results draw on "our forthcoming technical paper," and "We haven't begun full audits of this scale yet" [Cammarata, 2026, https://already-clean.goodfire.com/]. Three replies within five days. Leo Gao (LessWrong, October 17): the essay is "nowhere near a sufficient argument that we understand most of what neurons are doing," because neurons that look monosemantic from top activations may fire only on "a very specific subset" of the concept and weak activations may carry information [Gao, 2026, https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Dkh7pXKjoNWkkxQDz/neural-networks-are-not-already-clean]. Liv Gorton (October 20): the arguments "only work for the highly non-standard definition of superposition," and on rerunning the experiments "my results have been different than those described" [Gorton, 2026, https://livgorton.com/already-clean-thoughts/]. Neel Nanda of Google DeepMind, on X and in the LessWrong linkpost thread: "This is interesting work, but I don't think it's a significant update to the big picture of interpretability" [Nanda, 2026]. The essay does not mention monitoring.

Placement. Recorded in one paragraph because it bears on C2's premise and on nothing else in this record. Every lab's answer to the question of what its research agents are doing rests on reading transcripts and actions after the fact (8.34, 8.35); a result that most neurons in frontier-scale models can be read would change what monitoring could rest on. The result is preliminary, unreplicated, disputed by two independent reruns within a week, and its authors say the technical paper is not yet out. No rung, no tracker item, no date.

**Market.** Read at 10:34 UTC on October 25, the Polymarket contract "OpenAI or Anthropic announces recursive self-improvement by" priced October 31 at 3.7% and December 31 at 19%, down from 21.5% on October 19 (8. (8.38, read three minutes earlier, has 3.4% and 18%; the two readings are order-book prices minutes apart.)35) [Polymarket, 2026].

**Against the report's own sentences.** Seven places read as "nothing has happened" when set beside the sources above, and the bracketed notes proposed for each are in the summary part. The executive summary says "No lab claims the loop has closed," which is true, and does not say that the labs measure the loop as partly running below their bar. 7.1's rung 4 row says "Not demonstrated, not claimed." 7.6 and 7.7 are about the triggers and the date and need only a clause. 8.25's lead calls the Opus 5.5 card and METR's summary "the clearest statements yet, by people building the loop, that the loop does not yet close," which is the negative clause of two documents that each carry a positive one. 8.35's placement of the Argon report says the three instruments "all three say the same thing," which is true of the bar and not of the acceleration two of them report. 9.1 point 1 says what insiders expect "is not the closed loop (Rung 4)," and 9.2 condenses the Risk Report to "less than a factor of 2," mostly from causes other than AI, dropping its "key factor" clause. The talk slide that prompted this subsection was built from those sentences. The correction is to say both halves each time the threshold is mentioned: below the bar, and real.

**Tracker.** None of T1–T5 triggers. The bar is a doubling attributable to AI (Anthropic, RSP v3.4), a generational improvement in one-fifth the 2024 wall-clock time sustained for months (OpenAI's Critical, this report's T5), or progress "substantially accelerating from historical rates" (Google). No measurement reaches any of them. The measured acceleration below them is real and is the labs' own: Anthropic "significantly faster … but not yet by a factor of 2," with internal measures that "have moved" and a per-person uplift of about 4×; METR about 1.5×, with a 30% chance of 2×; Google a point estimate 30% higher after April inside an interval that includes no change, and a chief executive expecting "continued acceleration because of RSI" over twelve months; OpenAI inputs up severalfold and "the overall pace of progress likely won't keep pace with these specific metrics." T1: no OpenAI High rating and no Anthropic declaration; Anthropic's "may cross this threshold in the coming year" stands. T4: Google's GCI series and Anthropic's AECI are the two published series and neither shows a break at the bar; Recursive's halving of training steps on one stage and AlphaEvolve's 1% are single gains. T5: no lab reports a generation time; OpenAI's pause ended on October 1 (8.38). C1 untouched. C2: Goodfire's claim, if it held, would bear on what monitoring can rest on; it is preliminary and disputed. C3 untouched. C4 gains Pichai's "RSI loops," Clark's 「RSIの外側のループ」, "singularity" from two chief executives and a co-founder, and Recursive's mission sentence. The verdict of 7.7 stands: no primary document places the closed loop in December 2026 – March 2027, and the nearest dated statements are Clark's above-50% for the capability in 2027 with its use near zero, and Pichai's twelve months of continued acceleration.

**Reading for this report.** The record on acceleration has two halves and the labs wrote both. Per person, Anthropic's technical staff report about four times the output with its frontier model; at the level of the lab, Anthropic estimates the gain at under two and METR at about one and a half, and both say the measurement is uncertain, lags, and might cross the bar within a year. Google's index is flat inside a wide interval and Google's chief executive expects acceleration. OpenAI publishes the inputs and withholds the output. The written bar in every case is a doubling or more attributable to the AI, and no lab has shown that; a report that stopped there would be reporting the labs' thresholds and not their measurements. The outside measurements split the way they have all year: strong where a verifier is exact, weak where the question is open, and the one working scientist in this record is paced by review and publication, not by ideas. The accounts around the measurements, Piper's charts, Clark's Tokyo probability, Pichai's loops, Altman's singularity, Recursive's launch, each use the loop's name for the condition the measurements describe, and none gives a date. The honest sentence for the talk slide, and for this report's summary, is the one Anthropic wrote in August: significantly faster, not yet by a factor of two.

What to watch: the values and periods behind the internal measures Anthropic says "have moved," in the next system card or Risk Report; METR's fuller publication of the embedded assessment with the period its 1.5× covers; whether any OpenAI document states a lab-level multiplier or a generation time against its own one-fifth test; a transcript of Pichai's two interviews and any Google per-generation figure beside the GCI series; a second release of Anthropic's index and whether the September and October points continue the slope; a technical report from Recursive stating what the system did and what people did; Goodfire's companion paper and any replication of the Qwen3-32B audit; and a text or recording of Clark's Tokyo lecture.

[confidence: high on the Mythos Preview system card, the August Risk Report, the Opus 5.5 system card and the Gemini 4 Argon report (all PDFs, read by text extraction), METR's summary, the OpenAI post (read through a reader proxy, matching the Internet Archive text 8.10 used), the Preparedness Framework (PDF), the AlphaEvolve post, the Anthropic Institute pages, Piper's two articles, Import AI 477, Recursive's two posts, the Goodfire essay, Gao's and Gorton's posts, the LessWrong linkpost thread, The Conversation article, and the ITmedia article (page source); medium on Pichai's words from the Sources interview, which are coverage of an episode not watched, and on his Big Technology words, which are from the host's free post and the free part of a paywalled transcript; medium on Altman's July sentence, read in Fortune and The Conversation and not heard; the Rocktäschel two-year claim is unverified; the NHK item and WIRED's review were not read; the commissioner's referee-report account is his and unchecked; all lab figures are self-reports except METR's, whose evidence METR itself did not see; the reading of the ladder and of the report's own sentences is the report's; curly quotes rendered straight.]

### 8.40 Security as the first field where AI research produces a checkable result: php2shell found with AI by three teams in August and September and fixed by people in October, Japan switches off and the United States stays up, a16z's chart of critical vulnerabilities past 600 a month, Mandia's 90 zero-days, and what the labs did about their own models, August – October 2026

Nine items, all in one field, and none above rung 3. The report places AI's research strength on a verification hierarchy (3.4): results are strongest where a machine can check the answer. Security has the fastest checker after software tests, since an exploit runs or it does not, and the record of August to October 2026 is the clearest instance so far of AI doing research that produces a result (rung 3) outside AI research itself. Three teams found the same fourteen-year-old class of bug in PHP between August and September, each with a frontier model, on discovery dates the sources give differently (below); the fix took the PHP project and a coalition led by OpenAI two months; the release of October 21 went out after the embargo broke; Japan's prime minister asked the country to patch or switch off that night, and about 250 Japanese organizations did; no production compromise has been confirmed anywhere as of October 23. Around it, a16z published a chart of critical vulnerabilities at 21 vendors rising from under 100 a month to over 600, Kevin Mandia's company reported more than 90 zero-days at Fortune 500 companies, and the labs said what they had done about their own models: Anthropic kept Mythos-class models from general availability between February and June, rated a Chinese open model at its own restricted model's level and asked governments to test open models, and GPT-6 Astra remains the only OpenAI model rated Critical in cybersecurity. [Corrected, version 1.28: GPT-6.1 Sol's addendum of September 29 rates it "Critical capability in Cybersecurity" (8.28), and GPT-6.1 Terra's of October 26 does the same; Astra was the first of three; see 8.43.] None of this is the loop closing (rung 4), none of it gives a date for RSI, and the report says what it is evidence of instead. The items are placed against rung 3, T3, C3, the 3.4 hierarchy, and H1 to H5 and H7.

**The bug and its discovery.** On October 21 the PHP project released PHP 8.5.12, 8.4.27, 8.3.36 and 8.2.35 to fix "two critical vulnerabilities that can allow unauthenticated remote code execution," with the risk "highest for mod_php, with a demonstrated exploit where an unauthenticated request can trigger the attack before any application code runs" [PHP, 2026a, https://www.php.net/archive/2026.php]. The advisories give the two bugs their names and scores: "RCE via OOM corruption," CVE-2026-109618, and "RCE via stale global state," CVE-2026-109619, each CVSS v4.0 9.2. The first: PHP handles out-of-memory errors with longjmp, and when an allocation fails while request memory is only partly initialized, "the shutdown procedure can read or branch on invalid state." The second: a worker keeps global state pointing at a previous request's freed memory, and a later request can use it before initialization resets it. Both advisories credit the same four reporters, lcfr-hax, sagitzadik-wiz, tomash-areion and amyb-oai, and record that unauthenticated code execution "was validated over HTTP against mod_php with default settings" [PHP, 2026b, https://github.com/php/php-src/security/advisories/GHSA-w8hw-w9fx-j79w; PHP, 2026c, https://github.com/php/php-src/security/advisories/GHSA-6wmf-4rv5-p3rp]. Neither advisory nor the release note mentions AI, the name php2shell, or the embargo.

The lineage is in Calif's write-up by Ryan Austin. Stefan Esser "unearthed" the first PHP memory-limit remote vulnerability in 2004 and did not release his exploit; bug #78876 was reported in 2020; OSTIF and Quarkslab's 2024 audit "rediscovered the same OOM issue. However, exploitation was not proven, hence the bug was not fixed." On July 30, 2026, "OpenAI invited us to join its Patch the Planet initiative." By August 1 the team had the first OOM-fatal and an interruption point, and "discovered multiple different paths independently leading to RCE"; on August 5 Austin posted a hash of the finding; on August 11 Calif reported the first bug to PHP; a proof of concept followed on August 14 and PHP confirmed it on August 15; the second bug was reported on August 21. The page names no model and says no special harness or restricted-access model was used [Austin, 2026, https://calif.io/php2shell/]. OpenAI's post supplies the model: "Calif identified a critical flaw using GPT-5.6 Sol as part of our Patch the Planet initiative, with Wiz also independently discovering and reporting both vulnerabilities" [OpenAI, 2026a, https://openai.com/index/coordinated-disclosure-of-critical-vulnerabilities/].

Wiz's account is the third discovery. Its researchers found the first bug on August 19 "using Wiz Atlas, Gemini 4 Argon, and GPT-5.6 Sol," confirmed unauthenticated code execution against Drupal on August 20 and WordPress on August 21, and reported to PHP on August 24; PHP "acknowledged the reports and began building a coordinated fix" on September 21. Wiz says Gemini 4 Argon made "an invaluable contribution to finding the vulnerability," that "frontier models were critical to making this process practical," and that one researcher did in a week what "would have taken a world-class team months."

Its caveat is the one that governs the exploitability dispute below: the exploit required "adapting to each target's memory layout through repeated testing and debugging," so an attack that works on one deployment "may need substantial adjustment for another" [Tzadik, Tamari, Ohfeld and Schwartz, 2026, https://www.wiz.io/blog/php2shell-php-rce]. Sagi Tzadik's post of the day: "As expected in 2026, AI did most of the work here. This research began with a prompt targeting security flaws in PHP functions reachable from popular codebases" [Tzadik, 2026, https://x.com/sagitz_/status/2112901387122332066]. Calif's post the same afternoon: "Let's set the record straight: @lcfr_hax was the first to discover php2shell and develop a working exploit. See his August 5 tweet" [Calif, 2026, https://x.com/calif_io/status/2112914613297668583].

When OpenAI's own team found its version is stated three ways. Brockman: Calif "used GPT-5.6 Sol to find a version of this bug in August; our team found a more complete version independently in September" [Brockman, 2026, https://x.com/gdb/status/2112921529650688312]. The PHP Foundation: Calif reported on August 11, Wiz on August 24, OpenAI "independently reported the same root cause" with no date given, and on September 18 "OpenAI reported further attack vectors" [PHP Foundation, 2026, https://thephp.foundation/blog/2026/10/21/todays-php-security-release-and-how-were-strengthening-php-security/]. SecurityWeek, as coverage: "Calif and OpenAI independently reported the OOM-to-RCE issue on August 11" [Arghire, 2026, https://www.securityweek.com/php2shell-patches-land-as-first-exploitation-attempts-hit-sensors/]. The advisories' credit list includes an OpenAI handle, amyb-oai, and no date. This revision records the discrepancy and does not resolve it.

**The fix, and why it took two months.** The PHP Foundation's post of October 21 is the maintainers' own account. "We won't pretend otherwise: the delay was a structural failure, caused by both the security team's decision process and its lack of resources." The team, "operating on consensus, struggled to choose between these and feared the repercussions of shipping the wrong choice," and is moving to majority decisions. Security work "still depends on a small group of foundation-funded developers and a little volunteer help." Since July 1 the team published 15 CVEs and "worked through more than 100 reports that turned out to be invalid, duplicates, or not reproducible"; "The growth of AI-assisted research also means the PHP security team receives far more reports than before," and "a person makes every call and works on every patch." The post thanks Calif, Wiz, OpenAI Codex Security, OSTIF, Quarkslab, Alpha-Omega, the Sovereign Tech Agency, and "Alpha-Omega, OpenAI, and Anthropic for giving the team access to their tools and funding some of the security work," and asks companies to "sponsor the PHP Foundation at a level that reflects how much you rely on it," naming hosting providers, cloud platforms and "Governments and public institutions whose services run on PHP" [PHP Foundation, 2026].

The 2024 audit's auditor, Mihail Kirov, wrote on October 22 that the 57-day, five-person Quarkslab audit put two findings in its report, both pages blanked before publication, that a CVE was assigned to the more serious one and never published, that he reproduced the crash by hand and suspected a deeper problem but did not pursue it, and that "A finding parked in a queue is not sitting still." He adds that a re-examination that took a week in 2024 took a day with current tooling [Kirov, 2026, https://tolmo.com/blog/faster-than-anyone-can-fix-them/].

The disclosure broke early, in stages. The PHP project's advance notice of October 20 scheduled the release for October 21 at 14:00 UTC and reserved "the right to release earlier should details of either vulnerability become public" [PHP, 2026a]. The Foundation's post-mortem, as Socket reports it: around 15:00 UTC on October 20 the two CVE identifiers appeared on forums and social media with a wrong release time and a wrong CVSS score of 10.0, with no technical detail; around 20:00 UTC Calif "accidentally published a video" of its exploits working, deleted it, and copies spread; mitigation guidance shared with critical-infrastructure providers around 23:00 UTC may have leaked; and at 07:41 UTC on October 21 Chainguard published built images with the fix. The Foundation's words: Chainguard "unilaterally broke the embargo and published built images containing the fix," more than six hours before the coordinated release. Chainguard's chief executive, Dan Lorenc, on Hacker News: "I made the call to get these out early when exploits hit in the wild" [Gooding, 2026, https://socket.dev/blog/php-embargo-dispute, as coverage].

Cloudflare's rules went live at 03:02 UTC, "about 9 hours ahead of public disclosure," after work "since early October 2026" with the PHP Foundation, OpenAI and Wiz; the post names Calif, Wiz, OpenAI and Quarkslab as the disclosing parties and says the vulnerability "was leaked before the official release date" [Cloudflare, 2026, https://blog.cloudflare.com/php-vulnerabilities-waf-protection/]. Brockman's version: "the original target date was a October 22 release, but details started to leak early, so PHP's maintainers brought the release forward to October 21," and "a leak significantly before the patch was available would have been very impactful" [Brockman, 2026].

The timeline, from the primaries named in each row:

| Date (UTC unless stated) | Who | What | Source | 
|---|---|---|---|
| 2004 | Stefan Esser | First PHP memory-limit remote vulnerability; exploit not released | Calif write-up | 
| 2020 | Bug #78876 reporter | OOM bug reported to PHP | Calif write-up | 
| 2024 | OSTIF / Quarkslab audit (Kirov) | Same OOM issue rediscovered; "exploitation was not proven, hence the bug was not fixed"; written up as denial of service; a Foundation-contracted developer saw "the first signs" | Calif; PHP Foundation; Kirov | 
| 9 May 2026 | Linux kernel documentation (Tarreau, acked Kroah-Hartman) | Rules for AI-assisted reports enter security-bugs.rst; the document now says an AI-found bug "must" be treated as public | kernel.org; GitHub commit log | 
| 30 Jul 2026 | OpenAI | Invites Calif to Patch the Planet | Calif write-up |

| 5 Aug | lcfr-hax | Hash of the finding posted on X | Calif post; X |

| 6 Oct | a16z | Chart: critical vulnerabilities at 21 vendors from under 100 a month to over 600; Mandia interview | X | 
| 17 Oct | Amazon MadPot honeypot | Exploitation attempt from a commercial anonymization network | AWS | 
| 20 Oct ~15:00 | Unknown | CVE IDs on forums and social media; PHP posts advance notice and moves release to 21 Oct 14:00 | php.net; Foundation via Socket |

| 21 Oct 14:00 | PHP | 8.5.12 / 8.4.27 / 8.3.36 / 8.2.35 and the two advisories | php.net; GitHub |

| 22 Oct | Trend Micro ZDI | Six kernel vulnerability reports posted to public LKML; Kroah-Hartman asks why | Phoronix |

| 23 Oct 06:31 JST | 日本経済新聞 | 「PHP欠陥でサイト止めた日本、止めなかった米国」 | nikkei.com (lede only) |

**What the labs said on the day.** Brockman's X Article of October 21, "Towards securing the internet," is the fullest statement by a lab officer. The bug "allows any attacker to execute code on nearly all deployed PHP application"; it "has been present in PHP for 14 years, and a limited version of it had been discovered in 2024 and wasn't considered significant at the time." OpenAI has "in parallel" been fixing "several other significant bugs we've found, including a remote code execution that Node.js announced today, but with less collective attention due to bandwidth constraints across our team and partner organizations. We expect to find many more such issues over upcoming months." Then the sentence this report quotes for the asymmetry: the work "also was quite slow and manual," "both properties that won't scale for the agentic era. We are feeling the fundamental limitations of managing each bug in the classic way."

The proposals: "authorized defenders (such as government) scanning the internet for vulnerable hosts and either notifying or, in extreme cases, patching those systems remotely"; "Defenders need to have a tighter OODA loop than attackers, which we believe is possible especially if defenders have access to stronger models than attackers"; "It should thus be a national priority to ensure that we always have the most capable cyber models." He dates the $1 billion in Daybreak credits for frontline defenders to September 3, the Astra launch day (8.1), and closes: "we're entering a new phase of cybersecurity, where the models are able to find vulnerabilities with internet-scale impact" [Brockman, 2026].

OpenAI's post of the same hour is the institutional version. "Over the past two months, we led a coalition of maintainers, researchers, and security providers to address critical vulnerabilities in PHP"; "PHP's maintainers led the fixes and release, with our teams and partners like Wiz, Cloudflare, and others contributing research, technical support, and coordination." OpenAI "engage[d] with multiple parts of the U.S. government, including the White House's Super Intelligence Force, the Office of the National Cyber Director (ONCD), the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), as well as international partners." The forecast: "these bugs are among several significant vulnerabilities we've already found and are working to fix, and we expect similar findings to increase sharply in the coming months."

The questions it puts to the field: "When, if ever, should governments or other operators use a vulnerability to patch systems they own or are authorized to manage after losing administrative access? What safeguards should apply?" and "We can imagine an end-state of internet-wide always-on scanning for known vulnerabilities, and either notifying site owners when one is detected or patching the system." The page is undated; Brockman's post links it at 14:58 UTC [OpenAI, 2026a]. The Node.js release it refers to fixed ten vulnerabilities on October 21, four rated High; the Brotli decoder out-of-bounds write, CVE-2026-111497, which "can lead to a crash, disclosure of process memory, or remote code execution," is credited: "Thank you, to OpenAI Codex Security - Khai Tran and Sylvie Mayer for reporting this vulnerability" [Node.js, 2026, https://nodejs.org/en/blog/vulnerability/october-2026-security-releases].

Patch the Planet itself predates the window. OpenAI's page describes "a Daybreak initiative built with Trail of Bits to help maintainers strengthen the critical open-source software," using GPT-5.5-Cyber with Codex and Codex Security; Trail of Bits "identified hundreds of security issues and merged dozens of patches"; HackerOne and Calif "support vulnerability triage, coordinated disclosure, and additional discovery work"; the first projects were cURL, NATS Server, pyca/cryptography, Sigstore, aiohttp, Go, freenginx, Python and python.org. The page does not mention PHP [OpenAI, 2026b, https://openai.com/index/patch-the-planet/]. Coverage dates the launch to June 22, 2026, inside the Daybreak program, with a human engineer reviewing every finding before it reaches a maintainer [SiliconANGLE, 2026, https://siliconangle.com/2026/06/22/openai-expands-daybreak-patch-planet-full-gpt-5-5-cyber-release/, as coverage]. The model that found php2shell, GPT-5.6 Sol, is a general release; OpenAI's October card rates its successors "High capability in the Cybersecurity domain, but below Critical" (8.35), and GPT-6 Astra remains the one OpenAI model rated Critical, [corrected, version 1.28: one of three, with GPT-6.1 Sol and GPT-6.1 Terra; see 8.43] "able, with tools and access, to find previously unknown security flaws and develop new ways to exploit them" (8.1).

**Exploitation: attempts in millions, compromises none confirmed.** Amazon's threat-intelligence team found in its MadPot honeypot telemetry "a PHP2Shell exploitation attempt dating to Oct. 17, 2026," four days before disclosure, from "an IP address associated with a commercial anonymization network," and says "it remains unclear how the actor acquired exploit information prior to disclosure." Its lesson for security leaders: "For critical vulnerabilities in internet-facing systems, measure patch and mitigation timelines in hours. A 7-day or 30-day SLA assumes a delay that no longer exists," and "AI-assisted analysis makes it cheaper to turn a patch diff or advisory into a working exploit" [Amazon, 2026, https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/amazon-threat-intelligence-identifies-php2shell-exploitation-prior-to-public-disclosure-in-madpot/]. Cloudflare's Matthew Prince: "In the 10 hours before it was publicly announced, across the @Cloudflare network we saw thousands of attempts to exploit it. That jumped to tens of thousands in the 45 minutes post announcement, hundreds of thousands within 2 hours, and millions now" [Prince, 2026, https://x.com/eastdakota/status/2113079352682570051]. These are attempts matched by a rule, not compromises, and Cloudflare's post reports no breach [Cloudflare, 2026].

Whether the attempts work is disputed. WatchTowr saw first attempts as the patches landed and none successful: "Whilst nobody has been successful to date, we don't expect that will last long"; Hadrian said hours before the fixes, "We are now seeing php2shell exploited in the wild at scale" [Arghire, 2026, as coverage].

Marcus Hutchins, on October 23: anyone claiming to have "easily" generated a working exploit with AI is "either: a) a world-class exploit developer with an extreme case of imposter syndrome. b) currently looking at some very confident-sounding but wrong LLM output." The exploits on GitHub and in his honeypot traffic, "including one on GitHub with over 100 stars," are "session poisoning," a technique for local-file-inclusion bugs in PHP applications, "not PHP itself," sharing with the CVEs only the PHP_SESSION_UPLOAD_PROGRESS feature; "People are shoving loose details of the vulnerability into LLMs, the LLMs are keying in on the 'PHP_SESSION_UPLOAD_PROGRESS' string, then generating session poisoning code that has nothing to do with the actual CVE."

Worse, "'security researchers' are then using LLMs to triage these exploitation attempts, falling victim to the same hallucinations, then confirming there is ongoing mass-exploitation (there currently isn't)." The real exploit "is very difficult to exploit, and so far I've not seen anyone successfully get it working on more than a handful of PHP version with highly-specific configurations." His last line: "LLMs are useful tools in the hands of skilled professionals, but for others, they're often just a Dunning-Kruger accelerator" [Hutchins, 2026, https://x.com/MalwareTechBlog/status/2113463738833744047]. LiveOverflow's reply gives the other side in one prompt: "go through all wordpress images, extract the offsets and dynamically test the heap grooming strategy for it, then add it to php2shell.py" [LiveOverflow, 2026, https://x.com/LiveOverflow/status/2113457055822692727]. CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog, in the JSON released at 19:30 UTC on October 23 with 1,756 entries, lists neither CVE [CISA, 2026, https://www.cisa.gov/sites/default/files/feeds/known_exploited_vulnerabilities.json].

**Japan: switched off.** The prime minister's post of 21:55 JST on October 21, read with 100,176,959 views on October 25, verbatim: 皆様に、今夜に対応して頂きたい大至急のお願いです。多くのウェブサイトで使用されているPHPに関し、PHPのセキュリティチームが、重大な２件の脆弱性に関する注意喚起を行っています。公開しているウェブサイトでPHPを使用しているか確認し、今夜、10月21日午後11時（日本時間）に公開される修正パッチについて、可及的速やかに適用できるよう準備をお願いします。修正パッチを速やかに適用することが困難な場合は、例えばウェブサイト等を一時的に停止させることも、リスクを低減させるための有効策の一つと考えられます。 [高市, 2026a, https://x.com/takaichi_sanae/status/2112890642590564386].

The next afternoon she reported that her own site had been taken offline and restored: 昨日は、高市早苗公式サイトも、すぐに事務所からサイト管理者、サーバー管理者に連絡をしてアクセスができない状態にした上、対応を行ってきましたが、先ほど措置が終了し、アクセスを再開できるようになりました。状況によっては、サイトを一時的に停止することも重要だと私自身も改めて感じた次第です。 [高市, 2026b, https://x.com/takaichi_sanae/status/2113118173818224932]. ITmedia's headline the morning after: 「高市総理「大至急、PHPパッチ適用を」Xで異例の要請 エンジニアは評価「経営層に伝わる」」, naming BASE, ウマ娘 プリティーダービー, Shadowverse: Worlds Beyond and コドモン among services in emergency maintenance [ITmedia NEWS, 2026, https://www.itmedia.co.jp/news/article/2610/22/2000002483/]. NHK at 22:31 JST: “PHPでぜい弱性確認 速やかな対応を”情報処理推進機構 [NHK, 2026, https://news.web.nhk/newsweb/na/nd-20261021de63209].

The count comes from piyolog, the security blog that compiles operators' notices. Its entry, created 17:45 JST on October 21 and revised through October 24, reads as of noon on October 23: 各組織の告知で確認できたのは次の246組織で、業種別に示す。, with games 30, entertainment and music 33, e-commerce and reservations 27, hosting and IT services 29, municipalities 18, finance 8, government 3 and the rest across sectors.

Its exploitation table carries CISA's reading, 注意喚起の時点で、実環境で悪用が成功したことは観測していない, NCSC-NL's 攻撃が実際に成功したことは確認されていないとしている, Hunt.io's 成功した侵害は確認していない, and JPCERT/CC's caution that これらの痕跡がなくても攻撃を受けていないとは断定できないとしている; a footnote records that as of 14:00 JST on October 24 CISA's KEV listed none of the three PHP CVEs [piyolog, 2026, https://piyolog.hatenadiary.jp/entry/2026/10/21/174516]. The 211 figure a lead list for this revision carried for 01:00 JST on October 22 was an earlier revision of the same page and cannot be recovered; the Internet Archive holds no capture. The count is of organizations that paused or announced emergency maintenance, not of compromises; the only operator notice naming the CVEs among those read for this revision is Sakura Internet's, and the notices read report no unauthorized access.

The government's documents say patch, restrict, or stop. IPA's alert of October 22, 「PHPの脆弱性対策について(CVE-2026-109618、CVE-2026-109619：PHP2shell)」: 「利用環境に応じた暫定対策やアクセス制限を検討してください。」 and 「外部からのアクセス遮断やサービスの一時停止が必要か判断してください。」, with no statement of confirmed attacks [IPA, 2026, https://www.ipa.go.jp/security/security-alert/2026/alert20261022.html]. JPCERT/CC's 注意喚起 JPCERT-AT-2026-0033 of the same day: 「本脆弱性が悪用された場合、PHPの実行プロセスの権限で任意のコードを実行される可能性があります。」, lists indicators (unusually long PHPSESSID cookies, suspicious multipart requests, memory-limit errors and HTTP 500s, worker restarts), and warns that absent indicators do not establish that no attack occurred [JPCERT/CC, 2026, https://www.jpcert.or.jp/at/2026/at260033.html].

The Nikkei's headline of October 23, 06:31 JST, is the comparison the talk used: 「PHP欠陥でサイト止めた日本、止めなかった米国 脆弱性は8月に発見」, with the lede 多くの日本企業がウェブサイトを一時停止した。一方、震源地の米国ではサイトを継続する企業が大半だった。先端技術を持つテック企業が政府と綿密に連携して対応した防衛体制の違いも大きい。 The rest, 1,762 characters, is behind the paywall and was not read; the Microsoft and Proofpoint figures a lead list for this revision attributed to it are not in the free text and are not used here [日本経済新聞, 2026, https://www.nikkei.com/article/DGXZQOGN22C6A0S6A021C2000000/, lede only].

**The kernel's rule.** The Linux kernel's documentation for reporting security bugs now says, under "What qualifies as a security bug": "If you resorted to AI assistance to identify a bug, you must treat it as public. While you may have valid reasons to believe it is not, the security team's experience shows that bugs discovered this way systematically surface simultaneously across multiple researchers, often on the same day. In this case, do not publicly share a reproducer, as this could cause unintended harm; just mention that one is available and maintainers might ask for it privately if they need it." A separate section, "Responsible use of AI to find bugs," opens: "A significant fraction of bug reports submitted to the security team are actually the result of code reviews assisted by AI tools. While this can be an efficient means to find bugs in rarely explored areas, it causes an overload on maintainers, who are sometimes forced to ignore such reports due to their poor quality or accuracy" [Linux kernel, 2026, https://docs.kernel.org/process/security-bugs.html].

The commit log shows the AI-report rules entering the file on May 9, 2026, in a series by Willy Tarreau acked by Greg Kroah-Hartman, with a further clarification on August 2; which commit carries the "treat it as public" sentence was not pinned [torvalds/linux, 2026, https://github.com/torvalds/linux/commits/master/Documentation/process/security-bugs.rst]. On October 21 and 22 Trend Micro's Zero Day Initiative posted six kernel vulnerability reports to the public mailing list, two of them remote code execution in SMB code at CVSS 9.0, and Kroah-Hartman replied to each asking about the public submission [Larabel, 2026, https://www.phoronix.com/news/LKML-New-Trend-Vulnerabilities, as coverage]. The rule is the kernel's prior answer to the question OpenAI's post asks: when AI finds bugs faster than embargoes can hold, the kernel treats the finding as already public. php2shell, embargoed for ten weeks, is the counter-case, and the embargo broke anyway.

**a16z's chart and Mandia's zero-days.** On October 6 a16z posted: "Cyber is having a moment. Across 21 major software companies, including Apple, AWS, Microsoft, and Google: Reported critical vulnerabilities never cleared 100 per month in four years. Since spring they've jumped to over 600 per month" [a16z, 2026c, https://x.com/a16z/status/2107523804184678485].

Coverage names the data source as Epoch AI [Dealroom, 2026, https://dealroom.co/news/talk-cJsHel27Z6M-cyber-risk-goes-parabolic-critical-software-vulnerabilities-top-600-a-mo/, as coverage], and Epoch's own note of July 2, by Luke Emberson, gives the series: CVEs from cve.org reported by 21 organizations chosen "to avoid capturing noisy submissions from less reputable sources" (Microsoft, Google, Apple, Adobe, Oracle, Cisco, IBM, Red Hat, Intel, AMD, NVIDIA, Qualcomm, Samsung, SAP, AWS, VMware, GitHub, Linux, Mozilla, Apache, OpenSSL); critical-only counts of 3 to 65 a month from 2022 to February 2026, then 57 in March, 98 in April, 142 in May and 371 in June; high and critical together "around 1,500" in June, "more than 3.5× the monthly record prior to Mythos' release."

Epoch's attribution is temporal and hedged: "The spike follows Anthropic's April announcement that Claude Mythos Preview could" find vulnerabilities autonomously; some of the rise is "almost certainly due to increased feasibility of discovery," and "the spike may also be caused in part by an increase in the amount of interest in discovering bugs"; the figures "do not include discovered but not publicly disclosed vulnerabilities," and Project Glasswing "claims to have found over 10,000 high- or critical-severity vulnerabilities" not yet individually disclosed [Emberson, 2026, https://epoch.ai/data-insights/cve-severity-spike]. The 600-a-month figure in a16z's post is for months after June and was not reproduced here from Epoch's table, which ends in June; it is recorded as a16z's reading of Epoch's data.

The post quotes a video interview of Kevin Mandia, founder of Mandiant and of Armadin, by a16z's David George. a16z's text: Mandia "had no plans to start another company until AI arrived and changed everything: in his words, 'everything I did is dead,'" and the conversation covers "why nation states hack like a sniper and AI hacks like a drone swarm"; the chapter list includes "16:40 90+ zero-days at Fortune 500s," "24:30 Why the next year in cyber won't be pretty," "26:30 How Mythos changed the threat" and "30:05 Open and closed models tied on offense" [a16z, 2026a, https://x.com/a16z/status/2107479755054518563].

The clip post quotes him directly: "When you have an AI-based attack, it'll find logic flaws rather than code flaws in custom applications. It'll exhaust all routes all the time"; "Armadin, since January of this year, we have found over 90 zero-days at customer sites, all in production"; "We've post-trained all our models with real red teamers, real folks that actually can develop exploits"; "We are black box coming from the internet"; "we're calling a CISO, usually within 48 hours, 'Hey, we've got remote code execution in your DMZ.'"; "That's not a pen test. That is like a real adversary coming at you" [a16z, 2026b, https://x.com/a16z/status/2107512494805963025]. The video was not transcribed here; "everything I did is dead" is a16z's quotation of him in its post text, and "won't be pretty" is a chapter title, not a verified sentence. Coverage reports Armadin raising $255 million at a $2.5 billion valuation, per the headline of a report not read here [SecurityWeek, 2026, search result only]. The 90 zero-days are the company's count of its own findings, unverified by any outside party; a16z's post is promotional for a company it interviewed, and it does not say whether a16z is an investor.

Two more Epoch notes bound the picture from the other side. On October 7, with Ipsos, Epoch found "no detectable increase in the share of US adults experiencing cyber incidents": 46% (90% CI 44–49%) reported at least one incident in the past twelve months in June and 45% (42–47%) in September, with the frequent-AI-user share moving from 53% to 51%; the analysis "does not identify which incidents were caused by AI and cannot estimate the effect of specific model releases" [Falkman Olsson and Edelman, 2026, https://epoch.ai/data-insights/cyber-incidents-flat-since-fable-5]. On October 16, Epoch's Cyber ECI placed the open cybersecurity frontier "roughly where the closed frontier stood 6–7 months ago": GLM-5.3 at 160.10 on three CAISI results, between GPT-5.4 (157.04, March 5) and Claude Mythos Preview (164.82, April 7), with Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra at 171; the authors note that CAISI did not name the US models it evaluated and that identities were inferred, and that GLM-5.3 has three results and Opus 5.5 four [Lee and Barry, 2026, https://epoch.ai/data-insights/open-closed-cyber-eci-gap].

**What the labs did about their own models.** Three items, two already in this record. First, Anthropic's September 29 assessment of GLM-5.3 (8.32), re-read for the sentence 8.32 did not quote. It reports that Zhipu's open-weight model "develops end-to-end exploits in 50 of 410 attempts" on ExploitBench against Claude Mythos Preview's 56 of 410; that despite its safeguards it engages with harmful tasks 64% of the time under a deceptive prompt, 92% with prefilled thinking tokens and 100% when abliterated, the abliteration costing "about 2,200 GPU hours at a computation cost of roughly $4,400"; and it states that "a critical threshold in freely accessible capabilities has now been crossed," while vetted defenders can reach "more advanced models, such as Claude Mythos 5.1," through trusted-access programs. It closes: "Governments should conduct safety testing on sufficiently capable AI models, including successors to GLM-5.3." The words "AI research" and "self-improvement" do not occur in the post, as 8.32 found [Fasano, Fleischer, McFaul, Xiao and Gallagher, 2026, https://www.anthropic.com/research/glm-5-3-and-the-spread-of-advanced-cyber-capabilities]. CAISI's reading of the same model was four months behind the US frontier (8.32); Epoch's, above, is six to seven; UK AISI's for GLM-5.2 was four to seven (6.3).

Second, Amodei's statement of October 21, item 3 of nine in his reply to Nathan Calvin (the whole reply is 8.38's item; this subsection takes only the hold). Verbatim: "we first had Mythos-class models in February but didn't make them generally available until June (when we had stronger cyber safeguards in place). We only offered them to a limited set of partners in order to focus on cyberdefense. It would have been much more economically rational to simply release the model to everyone. We likely sacrificed a significant chunk of a several-month model lead. But because of this, many companies secured their cyber infrastructure before the deluge of widely available cyber-capable models" [Amodei, 2026, https://x.com/DarioAmodei/status/2112732230124818891].

Anthropic told The New Stack the delay follows its Responsible Scaling Policy; that article's own timeline has Project Glasswing opening Mythos Preview to selected partners in April and about 150 more organizations on June 2, Claude Fable 5 on June 9 with added cybersecurity safeguards, and a June 12 export-control directive taking both models offline until June 30 [Caswell, 2026, https://thenewstack.io/anthropic-mythos-safety-pace/, as coverage; Constantin, 2026, https://thenextweb.com/news/dario-amodei-anthropic-race-to-the-top-nine-safety-decisions, as coverage]. The sentence is the first time a lab has given the length of an internal-only period for a frontier model and priced it in lead time; it is C3 evidence by the lab's own hand, and Calvin's item 4, "use of internal models far better than are available to the public," is the question it answers in part [Calvin, 2026, https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2112295219765137570].

Third, the access structure both labs sell. Anthropic's Cyber Verification Program, expanded October 6, has three tiers, each with "Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5.5, Claude Mythos 5.1, and future models": Defense Access, where in Anthropic's CyScenarioBench test 46 of 50 trials were blocked at some point; Red Team Access, with no blocks and 34 of 50 tasks completed, "matching the 67.6% no-safeguards rate"; and Specialized Access, reviewed "with the US government." It reports that Glasswing partners found "at least 129,000 verified vulnerabilities between April and July 2026," Anthropic's open-source scanning 5,500 more by October, over 33,000 of the 129,000 critical or high, and says "Cybersecurity is inherently dual use" [Anthropic, 2026a, https://www.anthropic.com/news/cyber-verification-program].

The Anthropic Cyber Mission of October 8 adds an OSS Scanner for open-source projects and a Critical Infrastructure Defense Program with eleven founding partners, and states the asymmetry in its own words: "Highly cyber-capable AI models are widely available to attackers now"; "in two years, AI will favor defense," but "in the near term, that may not be true"; in Glasswing, months often passed between discovery and fix [Anthropic, 2026b, https://www.anthropic.com/news/anthropic-cyber-mission]. OpenAI's counterparts are Daybreak, Trusted Access for Cyber, the $1 billion in credits (8.1) and Patch the Planet.

Anthropic's chief information security officer, Jason Clinton, on October 22: "as bad as things have gotten in the past 4 days, they are going to get a lot worse before they get better. security leaders, never let a crisis go to waste: please go to your boards and ask for help right now. not for ai, for the basics. (we need to hold on for ~18 months.)", quoting his own post of August 20, "The vulnerability tsunami is coming in just a few months and there won't be enough time to patch critical infrastructure" [Clinton, 2026a, https://x.com/JasonDClinton/status/2113325263157395456; Clinton, 2026b, https://x.com/JasonDClinton/status/2090543566682718542]. Japan's AI Safety Institute's ExploitBench notes of October 2 (8.34), which ran Opus 4.8 with the vendor's real-time safeguards off and found GLM-5.2 well below the Opus models, remain the one government measurement in this record with the model named and the condition stated.

**Placement.** The rung first. Finding a fourteen-year-old bug class in the world's most deployed web language and building a working exploit is research that produces a result a person or a machine can check (rung 3), of the narrow kind 3.4 describes: the verifier is exact and fast, the exploit runs or it does not. It sits beside the mathematics release of 8.35 and the kernels of 3.3 as rung 3 outside AI research, with one difference. In mathematics a formal verifier covered 42% of results and three fell in a day; here the verifier covered everything the discoverers claimed (PHP confirmed Calif's proof of concept within a day) and the dispute is not about whether the result is real but about how far it generalizes across deployments, which is Wiz's own caveat and Hutchins's point.

This harmonizes two earlier placements: 8.1 called Astra's Critical rating "Rung-2-adjacent autonomy in a domain with real-world verifiers," and 8.34 placed Japan's ExploitBench measurement at "rung 1 for this report." Benchmark scores on known vulnerabilities measure capability at rungs 1 and 2; a novel vulnerability found in deployed software and confirmed by its maintainers is a rung-3 result. The three findings in August were by three teams with three model combinations (GPT-5.6 Sol; GPT-5.6 Sol again, by OpenAI's own account; Wiz Atlas with Gemini 4 Argon and GPT-5.6 Sol), which is the "simultaneously across multiple researchers" the kernel's rule describes, and it is as close as this record comes to a replication of a research result, with the caveat that the three teams shared the Patch the Planet orbit and the same base model in two of three cases.

For the tracker, T3 is touched and not triggered: this is not venue research, it is not AI research, and it is not a Kirgis replication; Kirgis asked agents to do the research behind two papers and found them doing the engineering and not the research. php2shell is engineering of the highest order with the question supplied by a human (the audit's 2024 finding, the 2004 lineage, the choice of PHP as "a high-impact target").

C3 is touched twice: Amodei's February-to-June hold, the first lab-stated length and price of an internal-only period, and the tiered access programs through which Mythos 5.1 reaches only vetted defenders while GLM-5.3 is a download. T1 is unchanged: OpenAI's cybersecurity ratings run Critical for Astra and High for the October Sol and Luna while AI Self-Improvement stays below High [corrected, version 1.28: and Critical for GPT-6.1 Sol (8.28) and GPT-6.1 Terra; see 8.43], which is the clearest instance in the record of two capability tracks measured on the same models moving apart. No date.

The 3.4 hierarchy is confirmed and extended. Software first, then mathematics, then security, is the order the report has used by verifier speed; this window supplies the security case with numbers.

What it adds is the cost on the human side of the checker. Three AI-assisted discoveries in three weeks; a ten-week fix by a consensus team of a few paid developers; an embargo that broke in four stages; a prime minister posting at 21:55; 246 organizations switching off. Brockman's own words, "quite slow and manual," "won't scale for the agentic era," are the asymmetry stated by the party that produced it. The verification hierarchy predicts where AI results arrive first; it does not predict that the institutions on the receiving end keep pace, and in this field they did not. The kernel's rule, that an AI-found bug "must" be treated as public, is one institution's adaptation; OpenAI's proposal of government scanning and remote patching is another, not adopted anywhere.

Both halves of the record, with their numbers. The positive half: three independent discoveries with frontier models in August; Wiz's one researcher in a week against "a world-class team months"; Epoch's critical-only series at 21 vendors from 98 in April to 371 in June and a16z's reading of over 600 since; Anthropic's 129,000 verified vulnerabilities from Glasswing partners in four months; Armadin's 90 zero-days by its own count.

The negative half: no confirmed production compromise as of October 23 by CISA, piyolog's five sensor sources or Hutchins's honeypots; the public exploits mostly hallucinated and the real one working on "a handful of PHP version"; US adults reporting cybersecurity incidents at 46% then 45%, unchanged within the interval; the fix human, slow, and two months long. None of it bears on the labs' written bar for the loop, a doubling of AI research pace attributable to the AI (8.25, 8.35). Security is the field where AI's research result is most checkable and least contested, and the capability it shows is the one the labs rate highest; the loop closing (rung 4) is measured on a different track, and that track has not moved.

The incentives, stated. OpenAI found the bug with a model it sells, led the coalition, briefed the Super Intelligence Force, ONCD and CISA, and proposes that defenders have "stronger models than attackers" as "a national priority"; a lab whose model finds bugs in the world's software sells both the finding and the fix, and the post asking for feedback on a disclosure process is also a product announcement for Daybreak. Anthropic published that a free Chinese model matches its restricted one at exploits and asked governments to test open models (H2(a) in the exact form 9.4 named, mandatory testing above a capability line); its chief executive then said the company kept its own models from general availability for four months for cybersecurity reasons at a cost in lead, which is H1's cost-bearing evidence and H4's confirmation that frontier models exist months before the public sees them.
