CNET Japan, through Yahoo!ニュース at 17:20 JST on October 27: OpenAIによると、AIはこの仮説が誤りであることを示す証明を生成した。数学的な証明をコンピューターで形式的に検証するソフトウェア「Lean」でも確認したという, and ただし、今回の証明が直ちに実用的な計算速度の向上につながるわけではなく、OpenAIもさらなる研究が必要だとしている [CNET Japan, 2026, https://news.yahoo.co.jp/articles/f9fbb964b0086e7ebd44c9455f8f02cbf650b527]. XenoSpectrum, October 27, drew the distinction this report draws: ただし、専門家が検証に参加していることと、主張の正しさが確認されたことは区別する必要がある, and Leanで形式的な証明が検証されたという説明だけで、人間の研究者が意図するETHの反駁まで確定したと判断することはできない; it also notes a 2021 arXiv preprint that claimed to refute the counting version of ETH and was not accepted [XenoSpectrum, 2026, https://xenospectrum.com/openai-exponential-time-hypothesis-false-claim/]. Neither mentions Anthropic's paper, and no Nikkei, ITmedia or NHK item was found on either result; the Japanese items on Anthropic's paper were blogs.

**Threads.** OpenAI's feed from October 26 to 29 carries the Terra launch (8.43), the academic-communities post, eleven customer and partnership items, "How we detect frontier model misalignment" (8.43) and an updated Model Spec; nothing further on the three results [OpenAI, 2026f]. Anthropic's news index through October 30 carries no post on the ETH paper [Anthropic, 2026e]. The openai/math repository's commit history could not be read for this revision; the GitHub API refused the query as rate-limited, and the history file is used instead [OpenAI, 2026c].

**Tracker.** None of T1–T5 triggers, and the verdict of 7.7 stands. T3: untouched, and this is the closest the record has come to its terms without meeting them. The item asks for a replication of Kirgis et al. in which agents produce research accepted at a top venue; the two ETH papers are research produced by agents, on a problem in theoretical computer science rather than AI, accepted nowhere, and the human acceptance that is under way is an open examination with no venue, no referee and a stated refusal to certify. T1, T2, T4, T5: untouched; no rating, no horizon, no efficiency series, no generation time. C3 gains two entries: the OpenAI result comes from the unnamed internal model of 8.31 and 8.35, still unreleased, and the Anthropic result from "an internal research Claude model" that no card describes. C4: no change; neither lab, nor Fortnow, Aaronson, Williams or the advisory group, applied the term RSI or any of its relatives to the event. C1, C2: untouched.

**Reading for this report.** Two internal models at two labs produced, by the labs' accounts, a disproof of the same twenty-seven-year-old conjecture on the same day, October 16, and Fortnow and Aaronson, two senior figures in the field, expect it to hold. That is the positive half, and it is the strongest instance of AI doing research that produces a result (rung 3) in this record: a result the field believed false, found twice, machine-checked twice, with the conjecture's own authors organizing its review and two human proofs of the weaker statement inside three days. The negative half is in the labs' own documents. The result is asymptotic and begins to beat brute force, for formulas with 5n clauses, at 10^{10,000} variables; it concerns Boolean formulas, not AI research, and closes no loop; no person is known to have checked either Lean development; OpenAI's machine-checked bound is weaker than its paper's; the model that found OpenAI's result has no name, no token count and no attempt count, and the model that found Anthropic's has a class and a token count and no attempt count.

What the week adds to the report's method is the shape of acceptance: a letter that refuses to certify, a blog that calls the hypothesis "toast" before verifying it, a probability moved from 3% to 5%, and an advisory group that says the names doing the checking are shielding the company. For H4, both labs held a result for ten days and one of them, by its own account, had its model find it with no human input; for H5, one lab announced on a weekday morning with a news post and the other on the day it filed to go public, with a GitHub commit and an employee's post. No date for RSI, and nobody in this record offered one.

What to watch: the examination's first published findings and whether they confirm either proof, with the corrections; whether any person reads Anthropic's MainResults.lean and says the statement is ETH, and whether OpenAI's Lean bound is raised to the paper's; whether OpenAI names the model, the attempt count and the prompts for the three results, as the advisory group's pathway asks, and releases the Yang–Mills artifacts; whether Anthropic says which model "internal research Claude model" names and whether it is the "Model 2" of 8.38; the conference OpenAI agreed to fund, who convenes it and who pays; whether either paper is submitted to a venue under STOC 2027's AI-methodology rule (Q54), and under what author line; whether the two labs state independently that neither saw the other's work before October 26; and the arXiv announcements of October 26–30.

[confidence: high on the OpenAI post (reader proxy, 12.8 KB; direct fetch 403; dated by the feed), the OpenAI preprint (PDF, 64 pages by its contents table), the openai/math README, history and Lean documentation (raw files), the Anthropic README, NOTICE, Challenge, Solution and comparator files (raw files at the commit), the Anthropic paper (PDF, 49 pages; provenance, magnitude and Appendix E quotations verbatim), the commit metadata and repository history (GitHub API, before the rate limit), the Hexagon record (reader proxy), the open letter (Google Docs text export), the Williams, Alpöge and Nelson posts (fxtwitter API, with profile descriptions), Fortnow's post and Aaronson's post and comments (reader proxy), the Kintali and O'Donnell PDFs, the AGMAI statements (reader proxy), the Simons Foundation page (reader proxy), Anthropic's news index (reader proxy), OpenAI's feed, the Hacker News items (Algolia API), and the Japanese pages (reader proxy); high that the TNW sandbox article is from July 21 and unrelated (page date and body read; OpenAI's July post read to its pause sentence); medium on the Scientific American and TNW articles as coverage (both read in full through the reader proxy; neither adds a primary this draft could not open); Williams's fourth post was not retrieved; the openai/math commit log was not read (rate limit); Hexagon's deposit timestamp is the record's own; em-dashes inside quotations rendered as commas; curly quotes rendered straight.]

### 8.43 Ratings and monitoring: GPT-6.1 Terra is rated Critical in cybersecurity and below High in AI Self-Improvement at 76.39% on OpenAI's debugging rubric, OpenAI describes four monitoring layers and says most of METR and Redwood's findings are patched with one fix in progress, Google ships Gemini 4 Carbon to cybersecurity defenders with no model card and no Frontier Safety Framework reading, and Pichai tells investors that AI is increasing Google's "training and launch velocity," October 25 – 30, 2026

Four documents, each a lab describing its own instruments, and none of them a tracker trigger or a date for RSI. OpenAI published a system-card addendum for GPT-6.1 Terra on October 26 that rates the model Critical in cybersecurity, High in biological and chemical capability, and below High in AI Self-Improvement, with a research-debugging score of 76.39% that sits between GPT-6.1 Sol's 75.52% and GPT-6 Astra's 78.05% (8.28). On October 29 OpenAI published "How we detect frontier model misalignment," the first description of the monitoring system that METR and Redwood Research red-teamed in July (8.38): four layers from activation probes to a network-log check, a 30-minute cadence for frontier runs, paging disabled for internal Codex traffic, and the statement that most of the red team's findings are patched with "one remaining fix" in progress, where METR had relayed OpenAI's claim that all were. Google announced Gemini 4 Carbon on October 28, four weeks after Argon by this record's dates, to a subset of cybersecurity defenders through the Fairwind Program, with state-of-the-art cybersecurity scores, no model card, and no Frontier Safety Framework report; the only safety report it links is Argon's. The same afternoon Sundar Pichai told analysts that "AI is helping us increase our training and launch velocity," that he expects "continued acceleration because of RSI" over twelve months, and that internal deployment of Argon is "one of the most meaningful internal productivity levers we have seen on software engineering." The items are placed against rungs 1 to 3, T1, T2, T5, C2, C3, C4, Q2, Q50, Q64, Q67, Q83, Q85, Q86, Q91, Q92, Q100 and H1, H3, H4 and H5.

**GPT-6.1 Terra: a cheaper model, the same ratings, and a debugging score above Sol's.** OpenAI's "Addendum to GPT-6 Astra System Card: GPT-6.1 Terra" is marked "Published October 26, 2026," and the launch post entered OpenAI's feed at 00:00 UTC the same day [OpenAI, 2026a, https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-1-terra; OpenAI, 2026f, https://openai.com/news/rss.xml]. The launch post describes "a fast, efficient model that combines detailed visual reasoning and precise computer use alongside clearer, more natural writing," whose "standard API prices are 50% lower than those of GPT-5.6 Terra and GPT-6.1 Sol," at "$1 per million input tokens, $0.05 per million cached input tokens, and $5 per million output tokens." On the computer-use benchmark Agents' Last Exam it "performs on par with GPT-6 Astra at only one-tenth its price per token." Its one safety sentence: "GPT-6.1 Terra meets the Critical threshold in cybersecurity under our Preparedness Framework, and maintains the protections developed for GPT-6 Astra" [OpenAI, 2026b, https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-6-1-terra/].

The addendum's Section 9 gives the three ratings together: "Under our Preparedness Framework, we are treating GPT-6.1 Terra as Critical capability in Cybersecurity, High capability in the Biological and Chemical domain, and below the High threshold in AI Self-Improvement." The safeguards are "the same safeguards stack" as GPT-6.1 Sol and GPT-6 Astra, "with probes updated to be compatible with the model," applied "consistent with our decision to adopt the same Preparedness determinations for both models." Section 9.3 is two sentences: "In AI Self-Improvement, GPT-6.1 Terra does not reach our High threshold," and a pointer to the Astra card. Section 9.3.1: "GPT-6.1 Terra achieves a mean rubric score of 76.39% on the internal research debugging evaluation, below the indicative High threshold. All models still solve only a subset of difficult debugging tasks that can take experienced researchers hours or days to resolve." Section 9.3.2: "KernelGen 1P measures a model's ability to understand hardware constraints, debug correctness issues, and improve performance. GPT-6.1 Terra scores 45.34%, below GPT-6 Astra and below the indicative High threshold." A footnote on the comparison values: "The comparison values for previously launched models that are shown here may reflect later versions of those models, and may vary from the values published at launch" [OpenAI, 2026a].

The Astra card describes what the debugging rubric measures. "The Internal Research Debugging Eval measures whether AI models can debug 41 real bugs from internal research experiments at OpenAI, where the original solutions took hours to days to debug by experienced OpenAI researchers," plus "6 alignment auditing-related tasks." OpenAI's reason for watching it: "Many debugging tasks also require searching through large quantities of information, but do not require novel infrastructure, which leads us to expect they may be an early bellwether for increases in research capability." Astra scored 78.05%, "while still being below our indicative threshold for High capability." KernelGen 1P "evaluates an agent's ability to write and optimize kernels for OpenAI first-party hardware," and success requires "avoiding invalid shortcuts such as host-side compute, benchmark spoofing, or overfitting to the grading harness." The Astra card gives no number for KernelGen in its text, and the GPT-6.1 Sol addendum says only that Sol "performs substantially better than GPT-6 Sol on kernel optimization, and performs comparably to GPT-5.6 Sol" [OpenAI, 2026d, https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/ai-self-improvement-capabilities; OpenAI, 2026e, https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-1-sol/preparedness]. Terra's 45.34% is the first KernelGen figure stated in text in this record, and the threshold it is "below" is not stated for either evaluation.

On cybersecurity the addendum restates the Critical definition, a model that can "identify and develop functional zero-day exploits of all severity levels in many hardened real-world critical systems without human intervention" or "devise and execute end-to-end novel strategies for cyberattacks against hardened targets given only a high level desired goal," and reports "an ACE-normalized mean capability score of 97.10% on ExploitBench," with the Astra card's caveat that the benchmark's historical vulnerabilities "raise a risk of contamination that may inflate the reported results." A line under the same heading reads: "GPT-6.1 Terra succeeded on 5 of 22 targets (22.7%), compared to 9 of 22 targets (41.9%) for GPT-6.1 Sol and compared to 10 of 22 targets (45.5%) by GPT-6 Astra"; the page does not say in that line which evaluation it reports, and 9 of 22 is 40.9%, not 41.9%. On training, the addendum says only that "GPT-6.1 Terra uses the same types of data and training as GPT-6 Astra"; it does not mention the pause of September 20 to October 1 or the resumed run (8.38) [OpenAI, 2026a].

Placement. T1 does not trigger: a fourth OpenAI release since GPT-6 Astra is rated below High in AI Self-Improvement (8.1, 8.28, 8.35), and this one restores the figure the October card omitted. The figure is the positive half: a model priced at half of GPT-6.1 Sol and a tenth of Astra scores 76.39% on the debugging rubric against Sol's 75.52% and Astra's 78.05%, so the gap between OpenAI's cheapest and most expensive frontier models on the one AI R&D measure it publishes for all three is under two points, with the caveat that the comparison values may be later versions. The negative half is the same as in 8.28: the bar is unstated, every model is below it, and the task is debugging, which OpenAI calls a bellwether and not research. For Q100 and the divergence 8.40 named, Terra is the third OpenAI model rated Critical in cybersecurity and below High in AI Self-Improvement on the same card; 8.40's sentence that "GPT-6 Astra remains the only OpenAI model rated Critical in cybersecurity" was wrong when written, since GPT-6.1 Sol's addendum of September 29 carries the same rating (8.28), and is corrected in the summary notes. For Q83, the addendum is silent on whether Terra trained under the resumed run, so the question stands with a third model added to it. Rung 1 to 2 capability measurement; no date. The incentive: a launch post that names the Critical rating and not the self-improvement one, which is the pattern of 8.1: the rating that sells is in the post, and the one this report tracks is in the card.
